\begin{exa}
\label{exa:newtontest}
Apply Newton's method to the test equation $x^3+4x^2-10 = 0$ with $p_0 = 1.5$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here $p_n = p_{n-1}-\frac{p_{n-1}^3+4p_{n-1}^2-10}{3p_{n-1}^2+8p_{n-1}}$, and
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rlll}
\hline
$n$ & $p_n$ & $|p_n-p|$ & $\frac{|p_n-p|}{|p_{n-1}-p|^2}$\\
\hline
$0$ & $1.5$ & $1.3\times10^{-1}$ & \\
$1$ & $1.373333333333$ & $8.1\times10^{-3}$ & $0.446$\\
$2$ & $1.365262014875$ & $3.2\times10^{-5}$ & $0.487$\\
$3$ & $1.365230013916$ & $5.0\times10^{-10}$ & $0.490$\\
$4$ & $1.365230013414$ & $<10^{-15}$ & \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The number of correct digits roughly doubles at each step: $2$, $4$, $9$, then full double precision. The last column settles at
$0.490$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:newtonquad} below explains this number: it is $\frac{f''(p)}{2f'(p)} = \frac{6p+8}{2(3p^2+8p)} = 0.4902$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:newtoncos}
Solve $\cos x - x = 0$ by Newton's method with $p_0 = \frac{\pi}{4}$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
With $f(x) = \cos x-x$ and $f'(x) = -\sin x-1$,
$$p_n = p_{n-1}-\frac{\cos p_{n-1}-p_{n-1}}{-\sin p_{n-1}-1}.$$
The iterates are $p_1 = 0.7395361335$, $p_2 = 0.7390851781$, $p_3 = 0.7390851332$, with errors $4.5\times10^{-4}$, $4.5\times10^{-8}$ and
$4\times10^{-16}$. Three Newton steps give full accuracy, against $32$ steps of the fixed-point iteration $p_n = \cos p_{n-1}$ for an error
of $10^{-6}$ (Example~\ref{exa:cosfp}).
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:newtonconv}
Let $f\in C^2[a,b]$ and let $p\in(a,b)$ satisfy $f(p) = 0$ and $f'(p)\neq0$. Then there is a $\delta>0$ such that Newton's method
generates a sequence $\{p_n\}$ converging to $p$ for every $p_0\in[p-\delta,p+\delta]$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Newton's method is fixed-point iteration with $g(x) = x-\frac{f(x)}{f'(x)}$. Since $f'$ is continuous and $f'(p)\neq0$, there is
$\delta_1>0$ with $f'(x)\neq0$ on $[p-\delta_1,p+\delta_1]\subseteq[a,b]$, so $g$ is defined and continuous there. Its derivative is
$$g'(x) = 1-\frac{f'(x)^2-f(x)f''(x)}{f'(x)^2} = \frac{f(x)f''(x)}{f'(x)^2},$$
which is continuous on $[p-\delta_1,p+\delta_1]$ and equals $0$ at $p$ because $f(p) = 0$. So for any $k\in(0,1)$ there is $0<\delta<\delta_1$
with $|g'(x)|\leq k$ on $[p-\delta,p+\delta]$. For $x$ in this interval the Mean Value Theorem gives
$$|g(x)-p| = |g(x)-g(p)| = |g'(\xi)|\,|x-p|\leq k|x-p|<\delta,$$
so $g$ maps $[p-\delta,p+\delta]$ into itself. By the Fixed-Point Theorem the iterates converge to $p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:newtonquad}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:newtonconv}, if $p_n\longrightarrow p$ with $p_n\neq p$, then
$$\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{p_{n+1}-p}{(p_n-p)^2} = \frac{f''(p)}{2f'(p)} .$$
In particular the convergence is at least quadratic.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Taylor's Theorem about $p_n$, evaluated at $p$, gives
$$0 = f(p) = f(p_n)+(p-p_n)f'(p_n)+\frac{(p-p_n)^2}{2}f''(\xi_n),$$
with $\xi_n$ between $p_n$ and $p$. Divide by $f'(p_n)$ and rearrange:
$$p_n-\frac{f(p_n)}{f'(p_n)}-p = \frac{f''(\xi_n)}{2f'(p_n)}(p_n-p)^2,$$
that is,
$$p_{n+1}-p = \frac{f''(\xi_n)}{2f'(p_n)}(p_n-p)^2 .$$
As $n\longrightarrow\infty$, $\xi_n\longrightarrow p$ and $p_n\longrightarrow p$, and the continuity of $f'$ and $f''$ gives the limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
The two theorems are \emph{local}: they guarantee convergence only if $p_0$ is close enough to $p$, and they say nothing about how close
that is. Far from the root Newton's method can behave badly, and in practice it is often started from a point found by a few steps of
bisection.
\end{note}

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:newtonfail}
Show how Newton's method fails for (a) $f(x) = x^{1/3}$; (b) $f(x) = x^3-2x+2$ with $p_0 = 0$; (c) $f(x) = \arctan x$ with $p_0 = 1.5$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
(a) $f'(x) = \frac13x^{-2/3}$, so $p_n = p_{n-1}-\frac{p_{n-1}^{1/3}}{\frac13p_{n-1}^{-2/3}} = p_{n-1}-3p_{n-1} = -2p_{n-1}$. From $p_0 = 0.1$ the
iterates are $-0.2, 0.4, -0.8, 1.6,\dots$: each step doubles the distance from the root $0$. The theorems do not apply, since $f'(0)$ does
not exist.

(b) $f(0) = 2$, $f'(0) = -2$ give $p_1 = 1$; then $f(1) = 1$, $f'(1) = 1$ give $p_2 = 0$, and the iterates cycle between $0$ and $1$
for ever (Figure~\ref{fig:newton}, right). The only real root is $p = -1.7693$, far from the starting value.

(c) $\arctan$ is very flat far from $0$, so its tangent lines cross the axis far away. From $p_0 = 1.5$ the iterates are
$-1.694, 2.321, -5.114, 32.30,\dots$, which diverge. From $p_0 = 1.3$ they are $-1.162, 0.859, -0.374, 0.034,\dots$ and converge to $0$. There is
a critical starting value $1.3917$ between the two behaviours.
\end{solution}

\subhead{The secant method}
Newton's method needs $f'$, which may be expensive or unavailable. Replacing $f'(p_{n-1})$ by the slope of the line through the last two
iterates,
$$f'(p_{n-1})\approx\frac{f(p_{n-1})-f(p_{n-2})}{p_{n-1}-p_{n-2}},$$
gives the \textbf{secant method}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:secant}
p_n = p_{n-1}-f(p_{n-1})\frac{p_{n-1}-p_{n-2}}{f(p_{n-1})-f(p_{n-2})},\qquad n\geq2 .
\end{equation}
It needs two starting values $p_0, p_1$ and only one new evaluation of $f$ per step. The formula is the same as that of false position,
but the secant method always uses the two most recent iterates, whether or not they bracket the root. It therefore gives up the safety of
bracketing in exchange for speed.

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:secant}
Apply the secant method to $\cos x-x = 0$ with $p_0 = 0.5$, $p_1 = \frac\pi4$, and to the test equation with $p_0 = 1$, $p_1 = 2$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rllrll}
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{c}{$\cos x-x$} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$x^3+4x^2-10$}\\
$n$ & $p_n$ & error & $n$ & $p_n$ & error\\
\hline
$2$ & $0.7363841388$ & $2.7\times10^{-3}$ & $2$ & $1.2631578947$ & $1.0\times10^{-1}$\\
$3$ & $0.7390581392$ & $2.7\times10^{-5}$ & $3$ & $1.3388278388$ & $2.6\times10^{-2}$\\
$4$ & $0.7390851493$ & $1.6\times10^{-8}$ & $4$ & $1.3666163947$ & $1.4\times10^{-3}$\\
$5$ & $0.7390851332$ & $9.6\times10^{-14}$ & $5$ & $1.3652119026$ & $1.8\times10^{-5}$\\
 & & & $6$ & $1.3652300011$ & $1.2\times10^{-8}$\\
 & & & $7$ & $1.3652300134$ & $1.1\times10^{-13}$\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The secant method takes one or two more steps than Newton's method, but each step costs one evaluation of $f$ instead of one of $f$ and
one of $f'$. On the test problem its first two steps coincide with false position (Example~\ref{exa:falsepos}); from the third step on it
uses the two latest points, leaves the fixed end point behind, and accelerates: the number of correct digits grows by a factor of about
$1.6$ per step.
\end{solution}

To analyse the secant method we use the \textbf{divided differences}
$$f[x_0,x_1] = \frac{f(x_1)-f(x_0)}{x_1-x_0},\qquad f[x_0,x_1,x_2] = \frac{f[x_1,x_2]-f[x_0,x_1]}{x_2-x_0},$$
which reappear in interpolation (Section~\ref{sec:divdiff}).

\begin{lem}
\label{lem:dd2}
If $f\in C^2$ on an interval containing distinct points $x_0,x_1,x_2$, then $f[x_0,x_1,x_2] = \frac12f''(\xi)$ for some $\xi$ in that
interval.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Let $q(x) = f(x_0)+f[x_0,x_1](x-x_0)+f[x_0,x_1,x_2](x-x_0)(x-x_1)$. Direct substitution shows $q(x_0) = f(x_0)$ and $q(x_1) = f(x_1)$, and
a short calculation using the definitions shows $q(x_2) = f(x_2)$. So $f-q$ vanishes at three distinct points, and by the Generalized
Rolle's Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:genrolle}) $(f-q)''(\xi) = f''(\xi)-2f[x_0,x_1,x_2] = 0$ for some $\xi$.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:secant}
Let $f\in C^2$ near a simple root $p$ ($f(p) = 0\neq f'(p)$), and let the secant iterates $p_n$ converge to $p$, with $e_n = p_n-p\neq0$.
Then
$$e_{n+1} = \frac{f[p_{n-1},p_n,p]}{f[p_{n-1},p_n]}\,e_ne_{n-1},\qquad\text{so}\qquad e_{n+1}\approx\frac{f''(p)}{2f'(p)}\,e_ne_{n-1}$$
for large $n$, and the order of convergence is $\alpha = \frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}\approx1.618$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
In divided-difference notation the method is $p_{n+1} = p_n-\frac{f(p_n)}{f[p_{n-1},p_n]}$. Since $f(p) = 0$, $f(p_n) = f[p_n,p]\,e_n$, and
$$e_{n+1} = e_n-\frac{f[p_n,p]}{f[p_{n-1},p_n]}e_n = \frac{f[p_{n-1},p_n]-f[p_n,p]}{f[p_{n-1},p_n]}\,e_n = \frac{f[p_{n-1},p_n,p](p_{n-1}-p)}{f[p_{n-1},p_n]}\,e_n,$$
using the definition of $f[p_{n-1},p_n,p]$. This is the first formula. By the Mean Value Theorem and Lemma~\ref{lem:dd2},
$f[p_{n-1},p_n] = f'(\eta_n)$ and $f[p_{n-1},p_n,p] = \frac12f''(\xi_n)$ with $\eta_n,\xi_n\longrightarrow p$, which gives the
approximation.

For the order, write $C = \left|\frac{f''(p)}{2f'(p)}\right|$ and suppose $|e_{n+1}|\approx A|e_n|^\alpha$. Then $|e_n|\approx A|e_{n-1}|^\alpha$, so
$|e_{n-1}|\approx(|e_n|/A)^{1/\alpha}$, and the relation $|e_{n+1}|\approx C|e_n||e_{n-1}|$ becomes
$$A|e_n|^\alpha\approx C A^{-1/\alpha}|e_n|^{1+1/\alpha}.$$
For this to hold as $e_n\longrightarrow0$ the exponents must agree: $\alpha = 1+\frac1\alpha$, that is $\alpha^2-\alpha-1 = 0$, whose positive
root is $\alpha = \frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}$. (A rigorous version of this argument, with explicit bounds, is in the texts by Atkinson and by
S\"uli and Mayers.)
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
Newton's method has order $2$ but needs two function evaluations ($f$ and $f'$) per step; two secant steps cost the same and reduce the
error with order $1.618^2 = 2.618$. Measured per function evaluation, the secant method is the more efficient of the two
($\sqrt2\approx1.41$ against $1.618$), which is why it, and the bracketed methods built on it, are popular in practice.
\end{note}


\subsection{Multiple roots and accelerating convergence}
\label{sec:multiple}

\subhead{Multiple roots}
Newton's method needs $f'(p)\neq0$. When $f'(p) = 0$ as well as $f(p) = 0$, it still usually converges, but only linearly.

\begin{defn}
A solution $p$ of $f(x) = 0$ is a \textbf{zero of multiplicity $m$} of $f$ if, for $x\neq p$, $f(x) = (x-p)^mq(x)$ with
$\lim_{x\rightarrow p}q(x)\neq0$. A zero of multiplicity $1$ is \textbf{simple}.
\end{defn}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:multiplicity}
Let $f\in C^m[a,b]$ and $p\in(a,b)$. Then $p$ is a zero of multiplicity $m$ if and only if
$$0 = f(p) = f'(p) = \dots = f^{(m-1)}(p),\qquad f^{(m)}(p)\neq0 .$$
In particular $p$ is a simple zero exactly when $f(p) = 0\neq f'(p)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
If the derivatives vanish as stated, Taylor's Theorem of order $m-1$ about $p$ gives $f(x) = \frac{f^{(m)}(\xi(x))}{m!}(x-p)^m$, so
$q(x) = \frac{f^{(m)}(\xi(x))}{m!}\longrightarrow\frac{f^{(m)}(p)}{m!}\neq0$. Conversely, suppose $f(x) = (x-p)^mq(x)$ with
$q(x)\longrightarrow L\neq0$. If some $f^{(j)}(p)\neq0$ with $j<m$, take the smallest such $j$; by what was just shown, $f(x) = (x-p)^jr(x)$
with $r(x)\longrightarrow\frac{f^{(j)}(p)}{j!}\neq0$, and then $(x-p)^{m-j}q(x) = r(x)$ would tend both to $0$ and to a non-zero limit.
So $f^{(j)}(p) = 0$ for $j<m$; and $f^{(m)}(p) = m!\,L\neq0$ by the first part.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:newtonmult}
If $p$ is a zero of multiplicity $m\geq2$ of $f$, then Newton's method, started close enough to $p$, converges linearly with
$$\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{p_{n+1}-p}{p_n-p} = 1-\frac1m .$$
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
We prove it for $f(x) = (x-p)^mq(x)$ with $q$ differentiable and $q(p)\neq0$. Then $f'(x) = (x-p)^{m-1}\bigl(mq(x)+(x-p)q'(x)\bigr)$ and
$$g(x) = x-\frac{f(x)}{f'(x)} = x-\frac{(x-p)q(x)}{mq(x)+(x-p)q'(x)} .$$
Hence
\begin{align*}
\frac{g(x)-p}{x-p} &= 1-\frac{q(x)}{mq(x)+(x-p)q'(x)}\\
&\longrightarrow1-\frac{q(p)}{mq(p)} = 1-\frac1m\quad(x\longrightarrow p).
\end{align*}
So $g'(p) = 1-\frac1m\in(0,1)$, and Corollary~\ref{coro:fprate} gives linear convergence with this constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:double}
The function $f(x) = e^x-x-1$ has a double zero at $p = 0$, since $f(0) = f'(0) = 0$ and $f''(0) = 1$. Apply Newton's method from
$p_0 = 1$, and then two remedies.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{Newton's method.} The iterates are
$$0.582,\ 0.319,\ 0.168,\ 0.0863,\ 0.0438,\ 0.0221,\ 0.0111,\ 0.00554,\ 0.00278,\ 0.00139,$$
each almost exactly half the previous one, as Theorem~\ref{thm:newtonmult} predicts with $m = 2$. Ten steps give only three correct
decimals.

\emph{Remedy 1: using the multiplicity.} If $m$ is known, the iteration $p_n = p_{n-1}-m\frac{f(p_{n-1})}{f'(p_{n-1})}$ restores quadratic
convergence (Problem~\ref{prob:mnewton}). With $m = 2$ the iterates are $1.6\times10^{-1}$, $4.5\times10^{-3}$, $3.3\times10^{-6}$,
$1.1\times10^{-11}$.

\emph{Remedy 2: modified Newton.} If $m$ is unknown, apply Newton's method to $\mu(x) = \frac{f(x)}{f'(x)}$, which has a \emph{simple}
zero at $p$ (if $f = (x-p)^mq$ then $\mu = \frac{(x-p)q}{mq+(x-p)q'}$). Since $\mu' = \frac{f'^2-ff''}{f'^2}$, this gives
$$p_n = p_{n-1}-\frac{f(p_{n-1})f'(p_{n-1})}{f'(p_{n-1})^2-f(p_{n-1})f''(p_{n-1})} .$$
The iterates are $-2.3\times10^{-1}$, $-8.5\times10^{-3}$, $-1.2\times10^{-5}$, $-4.2\times10^{-11}$, and then they stop improving.

The stall is a round-off effect, not a flaw of the method. Near a double root $f(x)\approx\frac{x^2}{2}$ is computed as the difference of
nearly equal numbers $e^x$ and $x+1$, with an absolute error of order $10^{-16}$; so $f$ cannot be distinguished from $0$ once
$\frac{x^2}{2}\lesssim10^{-16}$, that is $|x|\lesssim10^{-8}$, and no method can locate a double root more accurately than about the square
root of the machine precision. The modified formula, which divides by a difference of small quantities, loses a little more.
\end{solution}

\subhead{Aitken's $\Delta^2$ method}
Many sequences converge linearly. If $\{p_n\}$ converges linearly to $p$ and $n$ is large, then
$\frac{p_{n+1}-p}{p_n-p}\approx\frac{p_{n+2}-p}{p_{n+1}-p}$. Treating this as an equation for $p$ and solving gives
$(p_{n+1}-p)^2\approx(p_{n+2}-p)(p_n-p)$, that is,
$$p\approx\frac{p_{n+2}p_n-p_{n+1}^2}{p_{n+2}-2p_{n+1}+p_n} = p_n-\frac{(p_{n+1}-p_n)^2}{p_{n+2}-2p_{n+1}+p_n} .$$
With the \textbf{forward differences} $\Delta p_n = p_{n+1}-p_n$ and $\Delta^2p_n = \Delta(\Delta p_n) = p_{n+2}-2p_{n+1}+p_n$, the new sequence is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aitken}
\hat p_n = p_n-\frac{(\Delta p_n)^2}{\Delta^2p_n} .
\end{equation}
(The second form above is the one to compute with: the first subtracts nearly equal products.)

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:aitken}
Suppose $p_n\longrightarrow p$ linearly, with $\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{p_{n+1}-p}{p_n-p} = \lambda$, $|\lambda|<1$, and $p_n\neq p$ for all $n$.
Then $\hat p_n$ converges to $p$ faster than $p_n$, in the sense that
$$\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\hat p_n-p}{p_n-p} = 0 .$$
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Write $e_n = p_n-p$ and $e_{n+1} = (\lambda+\delta_n)e_n$ with $\delta_n\longrightarrow0$. Then
\begin{align*}
\Delta p_n &= e_{n+1}-e_n = (\lambda-1+\delta_n)e_n,\\
\Delta^2p_n &= e_{n+2}-2e_{n+1}+e_n = \bigl((\lambda+\delta_{n+1})(\lambda+\delta_n)-2(\lambda+\delta_n)+1\bigr)e_n .
\end{align*}
Hence
\begin{align*}
\frac{\hat p_n-p}{p_n-p} &= 1-\frac{(\Delta p_n)^2}{e_n\Delta^2p_n} = 1-\frac{(\lambda-1+\delta_n)^2}{(\lambda+\delta_{n+1})(\lambda+\delta_n)-2(\lambda+\delta_n)+1}\\
&\longrightarrow1-\frac{(\lambda-1)^2}{(\lambda-1)^2} = 0,
\end{align*}
where $\lambda\neq1$ ensures that the denominator tends to $(\lambda-1)^2\neq0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:aitken}
Apply Aitken's method to (a) $p_n = \cos\frac1n$, which converges linearly to $1$; (b) the fixed-point iterates $p_{n+1} = \cos p_n$,
$p_0 = 1$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
(a)
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rllll}
\hline
$n$ & $p_n$ & $\hat p_n$ & $|p_n-1|$ & $|\hat p_n-1|$\\
\hline
$1$ & $0.54030$ & $0.96178$ & $4.6\times10^{-1}$ & $3.8\times10^{-2}$\\
$2$ & $0.87758$ & $0.98213$ & $1.2\times10^{-1}$ & $1.8\times10^{-2}$\\
$3$ & $0.94496$ & $0.98979$ & $5.5\times10^{-2}$ & $1.0\times10^{-2}$\\
$4$ & $0.96891$ & $0.99342$ & $3.1\times10^{-2}$ & $6.6\times10^{-3}$\\
$5$ & $0.98007$ & $0.99541$ & $2.0\times10^{-2}$ & $4.6\times10^{-3}$\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The accelerated values are several times closer to $1$, and the advantage grows with $n$.

(b) From $p_0 = 1$, the first Aitken values are $0.72801$, $0.73367$, $0.73691$, $0.73805$, $0.73864$, with errors decreasing from
$1.1\times10^{-2}$ to $4.5\times10^{-4}$, compared with $2.6\times10^{-1}$ to $5.4\times10^{-2}$ for the corresponding $p_n$. The
iteration itself is untouched; the extra accuracy comes from post-processing three consecutive terms.
\end{solution}

\subhead{Steffensen's method}
Aitken's method can be built into a fixed-point iteration: take three iterates $p_0$, $p_1 = g(p_0)$, $p_2 = g(p_1)$, replace $p_0$ by the
Aitken value $\hat p_0$, and start again from it. This is \textbf{Steffensen's method}.

\begin{alg}[Steffensen's method]
\label{alg:steffensen}
To find a solution of $p = g(p)$ given $p_0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=Step \arabic*:,leftmargin=*]
\item Set $n = 1$.
\item While $n\leq N$: set $p_1 = g(p_0)$, $p_2 = g(p_1)$ and $p = p_0-\frac{(p_1-p_0)^2}{p_2-2p_1+p_0}$.
\item If $|p-p_0|<\varepsilon$, output $p$ and stop.
\item Set $p_0 = p$ and increase $n$ by $1$.
\item Output ``method failed'' and stop.
\end{enumerate}
\end{alg}

It can be shown that if $g'(p)\neq1$ and $g$ is smooth enough, Steffensen's method converges quadratically, without any derivatives.

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:steffensen}
Apply Steffensen's method to $x = \cos x$ with $p_0 = 1$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The successive Aitken values are
$$0.728010361468,\quad 0.739066966909,\quad 0.739085133166,$$
with errors $1.1\times10^{-2}$, $1.8\times10^{-5}$ and $4.9\times10^{-11}$. Each new error is roughly the square of the previous one times
a constant: quadratic convergence from an iteration that, used plainly, converges linearly with ratio $0.674$. Each Steffensen step costs two
evaluations of $g$.
\end{solution}


\subsection{Practice Problems}

\begin{problem}
Use the bisection method to find $\sqrt[3]{25}$ to within $10^{-4}$, by solving $x^3-25 = 0$ on $[2,3]$. How many iterations does
Theorem~\ref{thm:bisection} require?
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
$f(2) = -17<0<2 = f(3)$, so there is a root in $(2,3)$. We need $2^{-N}<10^{-4}$, that is $N\geq14$. Fourteen iterations give
$p_{14} = 2.92401$, and $\sqrt[3]{25} = 2.9240177$. The first steps are $p_1 = 2.5$ ($f<0$), $p_2 = 2.75$ ($f<0$), $p_3 = 2.875$ ($f<0$),
$p_4 = 2.9375$ ($f>0$), $p_5 = 2.90625$ ($f<0$).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Use Newton's method to compute $\sqrt6$, starting from $p_0 = 1$, and show that the method is Heron's method.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
With $f(x) = x^2-6$, $p_n = p_{n-1}-\frac{p_{n-1}^2-6}{2p_{n-1}} = \frac12\left(p_{n-1}+\frac{6}{p_{n-1}}\right)$, which is Heron's iteration
(Example~\ref{exa:heron}). The iterates are $3.5$, $2.607143$, $2.454256$, $2.449494$, against $\sqrt6 = 2.449490$. The first step overshoots
because $p_0 = 1$ is far from the root; from then on the convergence is quadratic and from above.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Early computers had no division. Show that Newton's method applied to $f(x) = \frac1x-a$ computes $\frac1a$ using only multiplication and
subtraction, and use it to find $\frac17$ from $p_0 = 0.1$. For which $p_0$ does it converge?
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
$f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}$, so $p_n = p_{n-1}-\frac{1/p_{n-1}-a}{-1/p_{n-1}^2} = p_{n-1}(2-ap_{n-1})$. With $a = 7$: $0.13$, $0.1417$, $0.14284777$,
$0.1428571422$, against $\frac17 = 0.1428571429$. Writing $e_n = 1-ap_n$ gives $e_n = e_{n-1}^2$, so the iteration converges exactly when
$|1-ap_0|<1$, that is $0<p_0<\frac2a$, and then quadratically.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Show that $g(x) = \frac{x^2+3}{5}$ has a unique fixed point in $[0,1]$, and find a bound on the number of iterations needed to approximate it
to within $10^{-5}$ from $p_0 = 1$.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
On $[0,1]$, $g$ is increasing with $g(0) = 0.6$ and $g(1) = 0.8$, so it maps $[0,1]$ into itself, and $|g'(x)| = \frac{2x}{5}\leq0.4 = k$.
By the Fixed-Point Theorem there is a unique fixed point, $p = \frac{5-\sqrt{13}}{2} = 0.6972$. With $|p_1-p_0| = 0.2$, bound~\eqref{eq:fpbound2}
requires $\frac{0.4^n}{0.6}(0.2)<10^{-5}$, that is $n>11.4$, so $12$ iterations suffice. (Near $p$ the slope is $g'(p) = 0.279$, so fewer are
needed in practice.)
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Use the secant method with $p_0 = 0$, $p_1 = 1$ to solve $e^{-x} = x$, and estimate the order of convergence from the errors.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
With $f(x) = e^{-x}-x$ the iterates are $0.61270$, $0.56384$, $0.56717036$, $0.5671433066$, $0.5671432904$, with errors $4.6\times10^{-2}$,
$3.3\times10^{-3}$, $2.7\times10^{-5}$, $1.6\times10^{-8}$, $7.9\times10^{-14}$. The exponents of the errors, about $1.3, 2.5, 4.6, 7.8,
13.1$, grow by a factor of about $1.7$, close to the theoretical $1.618$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:mnewton}
Let $p$ be a zero of multiplicity $m$ of $f$. Show that $g(x) = x-m\frac{f(x)}{f'(x)}$ has $g'(p) = 0$, so that this iteration converges
quadratically. Use it with $m = 2$ for $f(x) = 1-\cos x$ from $p_0 = 1$, and compare it with plain Newton.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
With $f = (x-p)^mq$, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:newtonmult}, $\frac{g(x)-p}{x-p} = 1-\frac{mq(x)}{mq(x)+(x-p)q'(x)}\longrightarrow1-1 = 0$,
so $g'(p) = 0$ and Theorem~\ref{thm:fpquad} gives quadratic convergence. For $1-\cos x$ the root $0$ is double ($f(0) = f'(0) = 0$,
$f''(0) = 1$). Plain Newton gives $0.454$, $0.223$, $0.111$, $0.0554$, $0.0277$: halving each time. The modified iteration
$p_n = p_{n-1}-2\frac{1-\cos p_{n-1}}{\sin p_{n-1}}$ gives $-9.3\times10^{-2}$, $6.6\times10^{-5}$, $1.3\times10^{-12}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Explain why Newton's method for $f(x) = x^3-2x+2$ starting at $p_0 = 0$ fails, and suggest a remedy.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
The iterates cycle $0, 1, 0, 1,\dots$ (Example~\ref{exa:newtonfail}). The real root is $-1.7693$, and $f$ has a local minimum at
$x = \sqrt{2/3}$ and a local maximum at $-\sqrt{2/3}$; starting values to the right of the maximum are pulled towards the minimum, where the
tangent is nearly horizontal. The remedy is to bracket first: $f(-2) = -2<0<2 = f(-1)$, and a few bisection steps on $[-2,-1]$ give a
starting value from which Newton converges quadratically (for example $p_0 = -1.5$ gives $-1.8421$, $-1.7728$, $-1.769301$, $-1.7692924$).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Show that Aitken's method is exact for a sequence of the form $p_n = p+c\lambda^n$ ($c\neq0$, $0<|\lambda|<1$): that is, $\hat p_n = p$ for
every $n$.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
$\Delta p_n = c\lambda^n(\lambda-1)$ and $\Delta^2p_n = c\lambda^n(\lambda-1)^2$, so
$\hat p_n = p+c\lambda^n-\frac{c^2\lambda^{2n}(\lambda-1)^2}{c\lambda^n(\lambda-1)^2} = p+c\lambda^n-c\lambda^n = p$. Aitken's method assumes
exactly this geometric model of the error, and removes the error completely when the model is exact; for other linearly convergent
sequences the model holds only asymptotically, which is why the improvement grows with $n$.
\end{solution}


\section{Zeros of Polynomials and Complex Roots}
\label{sec:poly}

Polynomials are the functions met most often in practice, and their roots can be found by the methods of Section~\ref{sec:roots}. Two
features make them special. They can be evaluated, and differentiated, very cheaply by Horner's method; and a polynomial with real
coefficients may have roots that are not real, which no iteration confined to the real line can find. This section therefore begins with
the complex arithmetic the methods need. (The full theory of functions of a complex variable is the subject of the course
\emph{Theory of Functions of Complex Variables} on this site; here we use only what the numerical methods require.)

A polynomial of degree $n$ is
$$P(x) = a_nx^n+a_{n-1}x^{n-1}+\dots+a_1x+a_0,\qquad a_n\neq0,$$
with real or complex coefficients $a_k$.


\subsection{Complex numbers and the roots of polynomials}
\label{sec:complex}

A \textbf{complex number} is $z = x+iy$ with $x,y$ real and $i^2 = -1$; $x = \operatorname{Re}z$ is its real part and $y = \operatorname{Im}z$ its
imaginary part. Complex numbers are added and multiplied by the usual rules of algebra, replacing $i^2$ by $-1$:
$$(a+ib)+(c+id) = (a+c)+i(b+d),\qquad (a+ib)(c+id) = (ac-bd)+i(ad+bc).$$
The \textbf{conjugate} of $z = x+iy$ is $\bar z = x-iy$ and its \textbf{modulus} is $|z| = \sqrt{x^2+y^2}$, so that $z\bar z = |z|^2$. Division
uses the conjugate of the denominator:
$$\frac{a+ib}{c+id} = \frac{(a+ib)(c-id)}{c^2+d^2}.$$
Every $z\neq0$ has a \textbf{polar form} $z = r(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta) = re^{i\theta}$, with $r = |z|$ and $\theta$ the angle from the
positive real axis. In polar form, multiplication multiplies moduli and adds angles: $r_1e^{i\theta_1}\,r_2e^{i\theta_2} =
r_1r_2e^{i(\theta_1+\theta_2)}$.

\begin{exa}
Compute $(2+3i)(1-i)$, $\dfrac{2+3i}{1-i}$ and the three cube roots of $1$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$(2+3i)(1-i) = 2-2i+3i-3i^2 = 5+i$. For the quotient,
$$\frac{2+3i}{1-i} = \frac{(2+3i)(1+i)}{(1-i)(1+i)} = \frac{2+2i+3i+3i^2}{2} = \frac{-1+5i}{2} .$$
For the cube roots, write $z = re^{i\theta}$; then $z^3 = r^3e^{3i\theta} = 1 = e^{2\pi ik}$ gives $r = 1$ and $\theta = \frac{2\pi k}{3}$,
$k = 0,1,2$. The roots are
$$1,\qquad e^{2\pi i/3} = -\frac12+\frac{\sqrt3}{2}i,\qquad e^{4\pi i/3} = -\frac12-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}i,$$
equally spaced on the unit circle.
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}[Fundamental Theorem of Algebra]
\label{thm:fta}
Every polynomial of degree $n\geq1$, with real or complex coefficients, has at least one complex root.
\end{thm}

The proof belongs to complex analysis (it follows, for example, from Liouville's Theorem) and is given in the complex variables course.
Applied repeatedly, it gives the factorization of every polynomial.

\begin{coro}
\label{coro:factor}
If $P$ has degree $n\geq1$, there are complex numbers $x_1,\dots,x_k$ (the distinct roots) and positive integers $m_1,\dots,m_k$ with
$m_1+\dots+m_k = n$ such that
$$P(x) = a_n(x-x_1)^{m_1}(x-x_2)^{m_2}\cdots(x-x_k)^{m_k} .$$
So $P$ has exactly $n$ roots counted with multiplicity. Consequently, if two polynomials of degree at most $n$ agree at $n+1$ distinct
points, they are identical.
\end{coro}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:fta}, $P$ has a root $x_1$, and by the factor theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:horner} below, with $P(x_1) = 0$),
$P(x) = (x-x_1)Q(x)$ with $\deg Q = n-1$. Repeating the argument on $Q$ gives the factorization. If $P_1$ and $P_2$ have degree at most $n$
and agree at $n+1$ points, then $P_1-P_2$ has degree at most $n$ and $n+1$ roots; by the factorization it must be the zero polynomial.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:conjroots}
If $P$ has real coefficients and $z$ is a root of $P$, then so is $\bar z$. Hence the non-real roots of a real polynomial occur in
conjugate pairs, each pair contributing a real quadratic factor
$$(x-z)(x-\bar z) = x^2-2\operatorname{Re}(z)\,x+|z|^2 .$$
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Conjugation respects sums and products: $\overline{z+w} = \bar z+\bar w$ and $\overline{zw} = \bar z\,\bar w$. Since the $a_k$ are real,
$$P(\bar z) = \sum_ka_k\bar z^k = \sum_k\overline{a_kz^k} = \overline{P(z)} = \bar0 = 0 .$$
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
Find all roots of $P(x) = x^3-x^2+2$, given that $1+i$ is one of them.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Check: $(1+i)^2 = 2i$ and $(1+i)^3 = 2i(1+i) = -2+2i$, so $P(1+i) = -2+2i-2i+2 = 0$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:conjroots}, $1-i$ is also a root, and
$(x-(1+i))(x-(1-i)) = x^2-2x+2$ divides $P$. Long division gives $x^3-x^2+2 = (x^2-2x+2)(x+1)$, so the third root is $-1$.
\end{solution}


\subsection{Horner's method and deflation}
\label{sec:horner}

To evaluate $P(x_0)$ we use the nested form already met in Example~\ref{exa:horner}:
$$P(x_0) = \Bigl(\cdots\bigl((a_nx_0+a_{n-1})x_0+a_{n-2}\bigr)x_0+\dots+a_1\Bigr)x_0+a_0 ,$$
which needs only $n$ multiplications and $n$ additions. The intermediate values do more than evaluate $P$.

\begin{thm}[Horner's Method]
\label{thm:horner}
Let $P(x) = \sum_{k=0}^na_kx^k$ and define
$$b_n = a_n,\qquad b_k = a_k+b_{k+1}x_0\quad(k = n-1,n-2,\dots,0).$$
Then $b_0 = P(x_0)$. Moreover, if $Q(x) = b_nx^{n-1}+b_{n-1}x^{n-2}+\dots+b_2x+b_1$, then
$$P(x) = (x-x_0)Q(x)+b_0 .$$
In particular $P'(x_0) = Q(x_0)$, and if $P(x_0) = 0$ then $P(x) = (x-x_0)Q(x)$ (the \textbf{factor theorem}).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Expand the right-hand side:
\begin{align*}
(x-x_0)Q(x)+b_0 &= b_nx^n+(b_{n-1}-b_nx_0)x^{n-1}+\dots\\
&\qquad+(b_1-b_2x_0)x+(b_0-b_1x_0) .
\end{align*}
By the definition of the $b_k$, each bracket $b_k-b_{k+1}x_0$ equals $a_k$, and $b_n = a_n$; so the right-hand side is $P(x)$. Setting
$x = x_0$ gives $P(x_0) = b_0$. Differentiating, $P'(x) = Q(x)+(x-x_0)Q'(x)$, and so $P'(x_0) = Q(x_0)$.
\end{proof}

The $b_k$ are conveniently arranged as a table, \textbf{synthetic division}: write the coefficients in the top row, bring down $a_n$, and at
each step multiply by $x_0$, write the product under the next coefficient, and add. Applying the same process to the $b_k$ (the
coefficients of $Q$) gives $Q(x_0) = P'(x_0)$. So one Newton step for a polynomial needs only two passes of Horner's method.

\begin{alg}[Horner's method for $P(x_0)$ and $P'(x_0)$]
\label{alg:horner}
Input: degree $n$, coefficients $a_0,\dots,a_n$, and $x_0$. Output: $y = P(x_0)$ and $z = P'(x_0)$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=Step \arabic*:,leftmargin=*]
\item Set $y = a_n$ and $z = a_n$.
\item For $j = n-1, n-2,\dots,1$: set $y = x_0y+a_j$ and $z = x_0z+y$.
\item Set $y = x_0y+a_0$. Output $y$ and $z$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{alg}

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:hornernewton}
Let $P(x) = 2x^4-3x^2+3x-4$. Use Horner's method to compute $P(-2)$ and $P'(-2)$, and then find a root by Newton's method from
$x_0 = -2$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The coefficients are $2, 0, -3, 3, -4$ (note the zero for the missing $x^3$ term). Synthetic division by $x_0 = -2$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|rrrrr}
 & $2$ & $0$ & $-3$ & $3$ & $-4$\\
$x_0 = -2$ & & $-4$ & $8$ & $-10$ & $14$\\
\hline
 & $2$ & $-4$ & $5$ & $-7$ & $10$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
So $P(-2) = 10$ and $Q(x) = 2x^3-4x^2+5x-7$. Dividing $Q$ by $x+2$ in the same way:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|rrrr}
 & $2$ & $-4$ & $5$ & $-7$\\
$x_0 = -2$ & & $-4$ & $16$ & $-42$\\
\hline
 & $2$ & $-8$ & $21$ & $-49$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
So $P'(-2) = Q(-2) = -49$, and Newton's method gives $x_1 = -2-\frac{10}{-49} = -1.795918$. Continuing in the same way,
$$x_2 = -1.742432917,\qquad x_3 = -1.738970235,\qquad x_4 = -1.738956257,\qquad x_5 = -1.738956256,$$
with $P(x_4) = 4\times10^{-4}$ and $P(x_5) = 7\times10^{-9}$. Direct differentiation agrees: $P'(x) = 8x^3-6x+3$ gives $P'(-2) = -49$.
\end{solution}

\subhead{Deflation}
Once an approximate root $\hat x_1$ has been found, Horner's method gives $P(x)\approx(x-\hat x_1)Q_1(x)$, and the remaining roots of $P$ are
approximately the roots of the \textbf{deflated} polynomial $Q_1$, of degree one less. Repeating this finds all the roots.

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:deflation}
Find all the roots of $P(x) = 2x^4-3x^2+3x-4$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Dividing by $x-\hat x_1$ with $\hat x_1 = -1.738956256$ gives the deflated cubic
$$Q_1(x) = 2x^3-3.477912513x^2+3.047937724x-2.300230374 .$$
Newton's method on $Q_1$ from $x_0 = 1$ gives the real root $\hat x_2 = 1.254881884$, and deflating again leaves a quadratic whose roots,
by the quadratic formula (in complex arithmetic), are
$$\hat x_{3,4} = 0.2420372\pm0.9262455i .$$
So $P$ has two real roots, $-1.7389563$ and $1.2548819$, and a pair of conjugate complex roots, as Theorem~\ref{thm:conjroots} allows.
\end{solution}

\begin{note}
Each deflation uses an approximate root, so the coefficients of $Q_k$ carry the errors of all the earlier roots, and the later roots
can be noticeably less accurate. Two precautions help. First, find and deflate the roots of smallest modulus first. Second, use each
root found from a deflated polynomial only as a starting value, and refine it by a few Newton steps on the original $P$.
\end{note}


\subsection{M\"uller's method}
\label{sec:muller}

Newton's method and the secant method approximate $f$ by a straight line. \textbf{M\"uller's method} approximates it by the parabola through
the last three iterates and takes the root of the parabola that is nearer to the last iterate. Because a parabola can have complex roots,
the method moves off the real axis on its own and can find complex roots of a real polynomial from real starting values.

Given $x_0, x_1, x_2$, write the parabola through $(x_k,f(x_k))$, $k = 0,1,2$, in the form
$$p(x) = a(x-x_2)^2+b(x-x_2)+c .$$
The three conditions give $c = f(x_2)$ and, with $h_1 = x_1-x_0$, $h_2 = x_2-x_1$, $\delta_1 = \frac{f(x_1)-f(x_0)}{h_1}$, $\delta_2 = \frac{f(x_2)-f(x_1)}{h_2}$,
$$a = \frac{\delta_2-\delta_1}{h_2+h_1},\qquad b = \delta_2+h_2a .$$
The roots of $p$ satisfy $x-x_2 = \frac{-2c}{b\pm\sqrt{b^2-4ac}}$ (the rationalized quadratic formula of Example~\ref{exa:quadratic}).
Choosing the sign that makes the denominator larger in modulus gives the root nearer to $x_2$ and avoids cancellation:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:muller}
x_3 = x_2-\frac{2c}{b+\sgn(b)\sqrt{b^2-4ac}},
\end{equation}
where, for complex $b$, the sign is chosen so that $|b\pm\sqrt{b^2-4ac}|$ is the larger of the two. The process is then repeated with
$x_1, x_2, x_3$.

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:muller}
Apply M\"uller's method to $f(x) = x^4-3x^3+x^2+x+1$ with (a) $x_0 = 0.5$, $x_1 = -0.5$, $x_2 = 0$; (b) $x_0 = 0.5$, $x_1 = 1$, $x_2 = 1.5$;
(c) $x_0 = 2.5$, $x_1 = 2$, $x_2 = 2.25$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
(a) First step by hand: $f(0.5) = 1.4375$, $f(-0.5) = 1.1875$, $f(0) = 1$. So $h_1 = -1$, $h_2 = 0.5$, $\delta_1 = 0.25$, $\delta_2 = -0.375$,
and
$$a = \frac{-0.375-0.25}{-0.5} = 1.25,\qquad b = -0.375+0.5(1.25) = 0.25,\qquad c = 1 .$$
Then $b^2-4ac = -4.9375$, whose square root is $2.2220i$: the parabola has no real roots. Both choices of sign give denominators
$0.25\pm2.2220i$ of equal modulus; taking $0.25-2.2220i$,
$$x_3 = 0-\frac{2}{0.25-2.2220i} = -\frac{2(0.25+2.2220i)}{0.0625+4.9375} = -0.1-0.8888i .$$
The method has left the real axis. Continuing:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rll}
\hline
$n$ & $x_n$ & $|f(x_n)|$\\
\hline
$3$ & $-0.1000000-0.8888194i$ & $3.0$\\
$4$ & $-0.4921457-0.4470307i$ & $0.76$\\
$5$ & $-0.3522257-0.4841324i$ & $0.18$\\
$6$ & $-0.3402286-0.4430356i$ & $1.6\times10^{-2}$\\
$7$ & $-0.3390947-0.4466565i$ & $1.1\times10^{-4}$\\
$8$ & $-0.3390928-0.4466301i$ & $1.9\times10^{-8}$\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The limit $-0.3390928-0.4466301i$ is a root, and so is its conjugate.

(b) From these real starting values the parabolas have real roots and the iterates $1.4063$, $1.38878$, $1.3893896$, $1.3893907$ converge to the
real root $1.3893907$.

(c) The iterates $2.2866683$, $2.2888169$, $2.2887950$ converge to the real root $2.2887950$.

So the quartic has the four roots $2.2887950$, $1.3893907$ and $-0.3390928\pm0.4466301i$, all found by one method.
\end{solution}

\begin{note}
M\"uller's method is not guaranteed to converge from every starting point, but in practice it rarely fails, which makes it a standard
first method for polynomial roots. Near a simple root its order of convergence is about $1.84$ (the positive root of $\alpha^3 =
\alpha^2+\alpha+1$), between that of the secant method and Newton's method, and it needs only one new function value per step.
\end{note}


\subsection{Newton's method in the complex plane}
\label{sec:complexnewton}

Newton's formula $z_{n+1} = z_n-\frac{f(z_n)}{f'(z_n)}$ makes sense for complex $z$ whenever $f$ has a complex derivative.

\begin{defn}
A function $f$ of a complex variable is \textbf{differentiable} at $z_0$ if
$$f'(z_0) = \lim_{h\rightarrow0}\frac{f(z_0+h)-f(z_0)}{h}$$
exists, where $h\longrightarrow0$ through complex values. It is \textbf{analytic} on an open set if it is differentiable at every point of the
set. Polynomials, $e^z$, $\sin z$ and $\cos z$ are analytic everywhere, with the familiar derivatives.
\end{defn}

\begin{thm}[Cauchy--Riemann equations]
\label{thm:cr}
Write $z = x+iy$ and $f(z) = u(x,y)+iv(x,y)$ with $u, v$ real. If $f$ is differentiable at $z_0 = x_0+iy_0$, then the partial derivatives of
$u$ and $v$ exist at $(x_0,y_0)$ and satisfy
$$u_x = v_y,\qquad u_y = -v_x,$$
and $f'(z_0) = u_x+iv_x$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $h\longrightarrow0$ along the real axis, $h = t$ real:
\begin{align*}
f'(z_0) &= \lim_{t\rightarrow0}\left(\frac{u(x_0+t,y_0)-u(x_0,y_0)}{t}+i\frac{v(x_0+t,y_0)-v(x_0,y_0)}{t}\right)\\
&= u_x+iv_x .
\end{align*}
Now let $h = it$ along the imaginary axis:
\begin{align*}
f'(z_0) &= \lim_{t\rightarrow0}\left(\frac{u(x_0,y_0+t)-u(x_0,y_0)}{it}+i\frac{v(x_0,y_0+t)-v(x_0,y_0)}{it}\right)\\
&= -iu_y+v_y .
\end{align*}
The two limits are equal, so comparing real and imaginary parts, $u_x = v_y$ and $v_x = -u_y$.
\end{proof}

For example $f(z) = z^2 = (x^2-y^2)+i(2xy)$ has $u_x = 2x = v_y$ and $u_y = -2y = -v_x$, while $f(z) = \bar z = x-iy$ has $u_x = 1\neq-1 = v_y$
and is differentiable nowhere. The Cauchy--Riemann equations explain what complex Newton actually does.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:newton2d}
Let $f = u+iv$ be analytic and $f'(z_n)\neq0$. Then the complex Newton step $z_{n+1} = z_n-\frac{f(z_n)}{f'(z_n)}$, written in real coordinates
$z_n = x_n+iy_n$, is exactly the Newton step for the real system of two equations
$$u(x,y) = 0,\qquad v(x,y) = 0 .$$
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Newton's method for a system $F(x,y) = 0$ with $F = (u,v)$ replaces $F$ by its linear approximation and solves $J\Delta = -F$, where $J$
is the Jacobian matrix of partial derivatives. By the Cauchy--Riemann equations,
$$J = \begin{pmatrix}u_x & u_y\\ v_x & v_y\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}u_x & -v_x\\ v_x & u_x\end{pmatrix} .$$
Multiplying a complex number $\Delta x+i\Delta y$ by $f' = u_x+iv_x$ gives $(u_x\Delta x-v_x\Delta y)+i(v_x\Delta x+u_x\Delta y)$, which is $J$
applied to $(\Delta x,\Delta y)$. So the system $J\Delta = -F$ says $f'(z_n)(\Delta x+i\Delta y) = -f(z_n)$, which is the complex Newton step.
\end{proof}

So complex Newton inherits everything proved for Newton's method: near a simple root ($f'(p)\neq0$) it converges quadratically, with the
same proof as Theorem~\ref{thm:newtonquad} (the expansion $f(z) = f(z_n)+f'(z_n)(z-z_n)+O(|z-z_n|^2)$ holds for analytic $f$). The theorem
also explains why the Jacobian of an analytic function is so special: it is a rotation combined with a scaling.

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:cnewton}
(a) Solve $z^2+1 = 0$ by Newton's method from $z_0 = 1+i$. (b) What happens from the real starting value $z_0 = 0.5$?
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
(a) $z_{n+1} = z_n-\frac{z_n^2+1}{2z_n} = \frac12\left(z_n-\frac{1}{z_n}\right)$. From $z_0 = 1+i$, $\frac{1}{1+i} = \frac{1-i}{2}$ and
$z_1 = \frac12\left(1+i-\frac{1-i}{2}\right) = 0.25+0.75i$. Continuing,
\begin{gather*}
z_2 = -0.075+0.975i,\qquad z_3 = 0.0017157+0.9973039i,\\
z_4 = -0.0000046+1.0000022i,
\end{gather*}
with errors $|z_n-i|$ of $3.5\times10^{-1}$, $7.9\times10^{-2}$, $3.2\times10^{-3}$, $5.1\times10^{-6}$, $1.3\times10^{-11}$: quadratic
convergence to the root $i$.

(b) If $z_0$ is real, every $z_n$ is real, since the formula involves only real arithmetic. The iterates
$-0.75, 0.2917, -1.5685, -0.4654, 0.8415,\dots$ wander along the real axis and never approach $\pm i$. To find complex roots of a real
polynomial with Newton's method, the starting value must itself be complex.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{exa:basins}
Apply Newton's method to $z^3-1 = 0$ from $z_0 = -1+i$, and describe how the root reached depends on the starting value.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here $z_{n+1} = z_n-\frac{z_n^3-1}{3z_n^2} = \frac{2z_n^3+1}{3z_n^2}$. From $-1+i$:
$$-0.6667+0.8333i,\quad -0.5087+0.8411i,\quad -0.4993+0.8663i,\quad -0.49999991+0.86602490i,$$
converging quadratically to $e^{2\pi i/3} = -0.5+0.8660254i$.

Figure~\ref{fig:basins} colours each starting point of the square $|\operatorname{Re}z|,|\operatorname{Im}z|\leq1.5$ by the root to which Newton's
method converges from it. Near each root the \textbf{basin of attraction} is a large simple region, as Theorem~\ref{thm:newtonconv} predicts.
But the three basins meet along an intricate boundary: every point of the boundary of one basin is also a boundary point of the other two,
and arbitrarily close to it are starting points that lead to each root. Starting values far from all roots can give unpredictable results,
which is the complex counterpart of the failures in Example~\ref{exa:newtonfail}.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Practice Problems}

\begin{problem}
Use Horner's method and Newton's method to find the real root of $P(x) = x^3-2x^2-5$ from $x_0 = 2$, and then use deflation to find the
complex roots.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
At $x_0 = 2$ synthetic division gives $P(2) = -5$ (row $1, 0, 0, -5$) and $P'(2) = 4$ (row $1, 2, 4$), so $x_1 = 2+\frac54 = 3.25$. Continuing,
$x_2 = 2.811037$, $x_3 = 2.697990$, $x_4 = 2.690677$, $x_5 = 2.690647$. Deflating by $\hat x = 2.6906474$ leaves
$Q(x) = x^2+0.6906474x+1.8582888$, whose roots are
\begin{align*}
x &= \frac{-0.6906474\pm\sqrt{0.4769938-7.4331552}}{2}\\
&= -0.3453237\pm1.3187268i .
\end{align*}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Verify the Cauchy--Riemann equations for $f(z) = e^z = e^x\cos y+ie^x\sin y$, and use them to find $f'(z)$.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
$u = e^x\cos y$, $v = e^x\sin y$. Then $u_x = e^x\cos y = v_y$ and $u_y = -e^x\sin y = -v_x$. So $f'(z) = u_x+iv_x = e^x\cos y+ie^x\sin y =
e^z$, as expected.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Show that $f(z) = |z|^2$ satisfies the Cauchy--Riemann equations only at $z = 0$. Why can Newton's method not be used to solve
$|z|^2-1 = 0$ in the complex plane?
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
$u = x^2+y^2$, $v = 0$: $u_x = 2x$, $v_y = 0$, $u_y = 2y$, $v_x = 0$. The equations $2x = 0$ and $2y = 0$ hold only at the origin, so $f$ is
not differentiable anywhere else, and $f'(z_n)$ in Newton's formula does not exist. (The equation $|z|^2 = 1$ is satisfied by the whole unit
circle, a curve rather than isolated points: one real equation in two real unknowns.)
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Find the three cube roots of $8$, and perform one Newton step for $z^3-8 = 0$ from $z_0 = -1+2i$.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
$z^3 = 8$ gives $z = 2e^{2\pi ik/3}$: $2$ and $-1\pm\sqrt3i = -1\pm1.7321i$. With $z_0 = -1+2i$, $z_0^2 = -3-4i$ and $z_0^3 = 11-2i$. So
$$z_1 = z_0-\frac{z_0^3-8}{3z_0^2} = -1+2i-\frac{3-2i}{-9-12i} = -1+2i-\frac{(3-2i)(-9+12i)}{225} = -1+2i-\frac{-3+54i}{225},$$
that is, $z_1 = -0.98667+1.76i$, already close to the root $-1+1.7321i$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Carry out the first step of M\"uller's method for $f(x) = x^3-x-1$ with $x_0 = 0$, $x_1 = 1$, $x_2 = 2$.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
$f(0) = -1$, $f(1) = -1$, $f(2) = 5$. Then $h_1 = h_2 = 1$, $\delta_1 = 0$, $\delta_2 = 6$, $a = \frac{6-0}{2} = 3$, $b = 6+1\cdot3 = 9$, $c = 5$.
So $b^2-4ac = 81-60 = 21$, and with the sign of $b$,
$$x_3 = 2-\frac{10}{9+\sqrt{21}} = 2-\frac{10}{13.5826} = 1.26376 .$$
The root is $1.32472$, so one step from three rough guesses has already reduced the error to $0.06$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Prove that if $P$ has real coefficients and odd degree, then $P$ has at least one real root.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
By Corollary~\ref{coro:factor}, $P$ has $n$ roots counted with multiplicity, $n$ odd. By Theorem~\ref{thm:conjroots} the non-real roots come in
conjugate pairs, with equal multiplicities (apply the theorem to the factors $(x-z)^m$), so they account for an even number of the $n$
roots. Since $n$ is odd, at least one root is real. (This also follows from the Intermediate Value Theorem, since $P(x)$ takes both signs
for large $|x|$.)
\end{solution}


\section{Direct Methods for Solving Linear Systems}
\label{sec:linsys}

Linear systems are the commonest problem of scientific computation; most of the later methods of this course (interpolation, splines,
least squares, implicit methods for differential equations) end by solving one. This section treats \textbf{direct methods}, which give the
exact solution in a finite number of steps if the arithmetic is exact. Their accuracy in finite-digit arithmetic depends on how they are
organized, and the main practical lesson is the need for \emph{pivoting}.


\subsection{Linear systems and matrices}
\label{sec:matrices}

A system of $n$ linear equations in $n$ unknowns,
\begin{align*}
a_{11}x_1+a_{12}x_2+\dots+a_{1n}x_n &= b_1,\\
&\ \,\vdots\\
a_{n1}x_1+a_{n2}x_2+\dots+a_{nn}x_n &= b_n,
\end{align*}
is written $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$, where $A = (a_{ij})$ is the $n\times n$ \textbf{coefficient matrix} and $\mathbf x$, $\mathbf b$ are column
vectors. For hand computation it is convenient to work with the \textbf{augmented matrix} $[A,\mathbf b]$, which carries the right-hand side
as an extra column.

Three \textbf{elementary row operations} change a system into an equivalent one, with the same solutions:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] multiply equation $E_i$ by a non-zero constant: $(\lambda E_i)\to(E_i)$;
\item[(ii)] add a multiple of equation $E_j$ to $E_i$: $(E_i+\lambda E_j)\to(E_i)$;
\item[(iii)] interchange two equations: $(E_i)\leftrightarrow(E_j)$.
\end{enumerate}
Each operation can be undone by an operation of the same kind, which is why the solution set does not change.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:invertible}
For an $n\times n$ matrix $A$ the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $A\mathbf x = \mathbf 0$ has only the solution $\mathbf x = \mathbf 0$;
\item[(b)] $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ has a unique solution for every $\mathbf b$;
\item[(c)] $A$ is nonsingular, that is, $A^{-1}$ exists;
\item[(d)] $\det A\neq0$;
\item[(e)] Gaussian elimination with row interchanges can be carried out on $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ for every $\mathbf b$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

This is the basic theorem of linear algebra, and it is proved in linear algebra courses. In this section we take it as known and
concentrate on (e): how elimination is carried out, what it costs, and how to keep its errors small.


\subsection{Gaussian elimination and backward substitution}
\label{sec:gauss}

\textbf{Gaussian elimination} uses row operations of type (ii) to create zeros below the diagonal, one column at a time. At stage $i$ the
entry $a_{ii}$, the \textbf{pivot}, is used to eliminate $x_i$ from equations $i+1,\dots,n$: equation $E_j$ is replaced by $E_j-m_{ji}E_i$
with the \textbf{multiplier}
$$m_{ji} = \frac{a_{ji}}{a_{ii}} .$$
After $n-1$ stages the system is \textbf{upper triangular}, and it is solved from the bottom up by \textbf{backward substitution}:
$$x_n = \frac{a_{n,n+1}}{a_{nn}},\qquad x_i = \frac{1}{a_{ii}}\left(a_{i,n+1}-\sum_{j=i+1}^na_{ij}x_j\right),\quad i = n-1,\dots,1,$$
where $a_{i,n+1}$ denotes the (modified) right-hand side.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95]
\fill[blue!5,rounded corners] (-0.3,-1.15) rectangle (15.6,3.05);
\draw[blue!45,rounded corners] (-0.3,-1.15) rectangle (15.6,3.05);
\node[font=\small\bfseries,text=blue!60!black] at (7.65,2.7) {Elimination clears one column at a time; back substitution then solves upward};
\foreach \k in {0,1,2,3}{
  \begin{scope}[xshift=\k*3.55cm]
  \foreach \i in {1,...,4}{\foreach \j in {1,...,5}{
     \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\z}{(\j<\i) && (\j<=\k) ? 1 : 0}
     \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\p}{(\j==\i) && (\i==\k+1) && (\k<3) ? 1 : 0}
     \ifnum\z=1 \node[font=\scriptsize,gray] at (\j*0.5,2.2-\i*0.5) {$0$};
     \else\ifnum\p=1 \node[draw=red!70!black,fill=red!15,circle,inner sep=1pt,font=\scriptsize] at (\j*0.5,2.2-\i*0.5) {$\ast$};
     \else\ifnum\j=5 \node[font=\scriptsize,green!40!black] at (\j*0.5,2.2-\i*0.5) {$\ast$};
     \else \node[font=\scriptsize,blue!70!black] at (\j*0.5,2.2-\i*0.5) {$\ast$};\fi\fi\fi

}

\node[font=\scriptsize,align=center] at (1.5,-0.55) {$[A,\mathbf b]$; pivot $a_{11}$};
